## Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP152

Proposal name:

Global Sub-national Climate Fund (SnCF Global) - Equity; Technical Assistance (TA) Facility for the Global Subnational Climate Fund (submitted separately)

Accredited entities:

Pegasus Capital Advisors LP (Pegasus);

International Union for Conservation of Nature and Natural Resources (IUCN)

Project/programme size:

Medium and Small

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

## 1.1 Impact potential

Scale: Uncertain

1. This assessment considers the medium-scale programme submitted to GCF through two independent funding proposals by two independent accredited entities (AEs). The programme has an equity fund establishment component (equity component), for which Pegasus Capital Advisors LP (Pegasus) is the AE to GCF, and a technical assistance (TA) component (grant), for which the International Union for Conservation of Nature and Natural Resources (IUCN) is the AE to GCF. Conceptually, the equity component of the programme is the leading component of the entire programme, and the grant component is a supplementary component mainly to ensure TA to the equity fund, the Global Subnational Climate Fund (SnCF Global), which will be established within this programme through the equity component.
2. The programme will be implemented through two different funded activity agreements: one between GCF and Pegasus; and one between GCF and IUCN. The reason for this implementation arrangement is the accreditation agreements between GCF and these two AEs. As clarified to the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) by the Secretariat, Pegasus is accredited for the management of equity-related funding proposals but is not authorized for grant management, while IUCN is accredited for grant management but is not authorized for management of non-grant proceeds from GCF. Accordingly, the proposed arrangement was developed to overcome the limitations imposed by the accreditation status of the two AEs.
3. The  programme  will  be  overseen  and  implemented  by  a  consortium  of  GCF  AEs  and program executing entities (EEs), each with diverse expertise and a long track record of operating success:
4. (a) Pegasus, 1  a GCF AE, will oversee management of SnCF Global and investment of the commitments of SnCF Global in individual projects;
5. (b) R20 - Regions of Action (R20) 2  will be an adviser to SnCF Global, and bring extensive experience partnering with subnational authorities in the field of low carbon and

1  Pegasus is an investment adviser based in the United States of America and registered with the United States Securities and Exchange Commission. Pegasus has been managing private funds since 1996, and has been investing in sustainability-focused investments for over 18 years.

2  R20 is a non-profit environmental organization founded in 2011 with the support of the United Nations. It is a coalition of subnational governments, private companies, international organizations, non-governmental

<!-- image -->

- climate-resilient infrastructure projects. R20 will manage the project implementing unit of the TA component;
- (c) Gold Standard 3  will advise SnCF Global on monitoring and reporting tools and metrics on climate change mitigation and adaptation impact, environmental and social management systems, and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDG). In addition, Gold Standard will ensure establishment of all necessary procedures for third-party certification of the impact of SnCF Global throughout the investment value chain in terms of climate mitigation, adaptation, ecosystem integrity, local rights, gender and the SDGs; and
- (d) IUCN, a GCF AE, will oversee and implement a TA facility established by a separate GCF grant (through a separate funding proposal). The TA facility is intended to facilitate a robust pipeline of bankable projects for SnCF Global.
4. SnCF Global will be established by the equity component of the programme submitted through a medium-sized funding proposal. It will consist of GCF equity at 20 per cent (USD 150 million, junior) of the total fund size, as planned in the funding proposal. The remaining 80 per cent (USD 600 million, senior) will be leveraged by SnCF Global from private (80 per cent share) and public investors (20 per cent share). In the final structure of SnCF Global, the private sector share will be about 64 per cent, and the remaining 34 per cent will be public money, including the GCF share. It is expected that the size of SnCF Global could be increased to up to USD 900 million after the fund starts full operation and is successful. 4 The GCF share will be delivered to SnCF Global through Pegasus, 5  a GCF AE. The equity funding proposal does not have cofinancing, but the term sheet states that Pegasus will be co-investing 1 per cent of the commitments of SnCF Global, at all closings. 6
5. Pegasus will be the fund manager responsible for: proper use of the resources of SnCF Global; ensuring compliance of SnCF Global activities with the funded activity agreement with GCF; ensuring that investments performed by SnCF Global respect GCF policies; reporting and accounting of SnCF Global; and ensuring proper alignment of TA activities with the interests of SnCF Global. BNP Paribas, also a GCF AE, will serve as fund custodian and depositary, provide fund administration services, and assist SnCF Global in its marketing and distribution efforts.
6. Past experience of the consortium members (in particular R20) shows that there is a significant investment gap at the subnational level, and an opportunity to realize climate change mitigation impact and economic returns as well. The programme aims to overcome the projectlevel barriers that lead to chronic underfunding of bankable mitigation projects at the subnational level through facilitating private sector involvement in the implementation process of GCF funding proposals. The AEs believe that anchor funding from GCF will unlock both public investors (multilateral development banks, development finance institutions, sovereign funds, etc.) and, even more importantly, private investors (pension funds, insurance funds, family

organizations (NGOs), and academic and financial institutions. Its mission is to accelerate subnational infrastructure

3  Gold Standard was established in 2003 by WWF and other international NGOs to ensure projects that reduced investments in the green economy to meaningfully contribute to the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).

carbon emissions featured the highest levels of environmental integrity and also contributed to sustainable standard for climate and sustainable development interventions. See &lt;https://www.goldstandard.org/about-

development. With the adoption of the Paris Agreement and the SDGs, Gold Standard launched a best practice us/vision-and-mission&gt;.

5  The GCF investment in SnCF Global will be held through a newly formed holding company managed by Pegasus on

4  The GCF amount will stay the same.

behalf of GCF. The holding company will be the 100 percent shareholder of GCF finance and will contribute the funds term sheet in annex 14.

to SnCF Global per the terms of the funded activity agreement. For details, see the structure chart in annex 29 and

6  The general partner (to be determined by Pegasus) and its affiliates, partners and employees will commit to commitment in the term sheet in annex 14.

investing in SnCF Global at least 1 per cent of the total commitments of SnCF Global. For details, see the Pegasus

<!-- image -->

- offices, private banks, philanthropies, high net worth individuals, and other institutional investors) that are willing to invest in green finance products but lack access to attractive investment vehicles to invest in at the subnational level. The AE (PCA) plans to mobilize public (20 per cent) and private sector (80 per cent) funding, in addition to the 20 per cent from GCF, at scale to de-risk subnational, mid-scale, infrastructure projects. The programme plans to cover all greenhouse gas (GHG) mitigation results areas, except transport.
7. For each individual project, SnCF Global will make equity investments into special purpose vehicles that manage the project. Each of the individual subnational projects in which SnCF Global invests will have its own capital structure. However, as a general structure, a 16 per cent share will be from leveraged private and public investments, a 4 per cent share will be from GCF, debt at the project level should be 60 per cent of the project cost, and the remaining 20 per cent should be co-financing from the project as equity. According to the funding proposal, such a partnership will contribute to mitigating the deficit in public development finance, which on its own is not sufficient to achieve the Paris Agreement's objectives and the SDGs - and investing from the private sector is needed. According to this general structure, USD 1 from GCF will leverage USD 25.
8. The equity funding proposal has four EEs:
- (a) Pegasus, a limited partnership registered in Delaware, the United States of America;
- (b) SnCF Global, not yet formed but expected to be an investment company in risk capital (known as an SICAR) and reserved alternative investment fund (known as an RAIF) registered in Luxembourg with a designated alternative investment fund manager;
- (c) R20, an association based in Geneva, Switzerland, under Swiss association laws; and (d) Gold Standard, a non-profit association based in Geneva, Switzerland, under Swiss association laws.
9. The TA component of the programme is to establish the pipeline of subnational-level, climate change mitigation investment projects for further financing from the equity component, SnCF Global. An additional function of this component is to develop special tools/protocols and methodologies for measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) and third-party certification for different sectors and different activities at the project and fund level. These tools will be used by investment actors to track the project's impact on infrastructure, synergies between mitigation results, and contribution to SDG targets. Another activity to be performed by the TA component is that of strengthening the capacity of the relevant stakeholders (project developers, subnational authorities and regional investors) to ensure their active participation in their respective projects' outreach activities, thus further promoting replicability and scalability.
10. The TA component of the programme is a small-scale funding proposal with a total cost of USD 28 million - with USD18.5 million from GCF, and USD 9.5 million in co-financing, with a significant share (USD 6) million from R20.
11. The TA funding proposal has three EEs: IUCN, R20 and Gold Standard.
12. The programme implementation period is 15 years, with a minimum whole programme lifetime of 20 years. The planned mitigation impact reported in the equity funding proposal is 3,881,722 tCO2eq annually, and 77,634,432 tCO2eq in total for the 20 years of the programme's lifespan. The programme does not plan to invest only in direct adaptation impact, but adaptation co-benefits of invested GHGs mitigation projects are anticipated and will be monitored. As an independent funding proposal, the TA component of the programme may not have any mitigation or adaptation impact, but it is crucial for successful implementation of the programme.

<!-- image -->

13. TAP considers that targeting currently underfunded subnational-level projects is an important activity, and supports this sector being a special focus for GCF. However, based on the information provided, TAP is not in a position to assess and confirm the final impact of the programme. In particular, the following barriers to assessment have been identified:
2. (a) The programme is submitted through two independent funding proposals: one is responsible for the TA component, and the other is responsible for the investment component (establishment of SnCF Global). It is the understanding of TAP that the proposed structure contradicts the guidelines that the draft decision contemplates as section on efficiency and effectiveness. The opinion of TAP is that, with such an implementation arrangement, the level of risk is high and the anticipated impact may
3. effective for programmatic approaches. Further details on this issue are provided in the not be high; (b) The programme does not have any legal regulatory mechanism defining the responsibilities and accountability between the two AEs and the GCF Secretariat; (c) Because the fund, at this stage, has been submitted as a 'blind pool', the GHG mitigation potential is estimated through theoretical funding proposals by sector; and (d) The remaining impediments are highlighted throughout the assessment in different sections.

## 1.2 Paradigm-shift potential Scale: High

14. The concept of the programme should be considered as decentralization of the GCF investment process. Establishment of SnCF Global (equity), which could leverage additional private and public capital (according to the funding proposal, USD 1 from GCF will leverage USD 25) and will target underfunded subnational public and private projects, is quite attractive, considering that subnational authorities/mayors in many developing countries worldwide have announced voluntary commitments for reducing GHGs from the territories under their authority. Such an approach, targeting subnational projects worldwide, could accelerate the removal of the existing barriers to subnational projects and facilitate the scaling up of underfunded high-risk activities at the subnational level through offering to the private sector maximum de-risking mechanisms with public money.
15. GCF has already approved several funding proposals from public investors (Asian Development Bank, European Bank for Reconstruction and Development, etc.) targeting subnational infrastructure projects. However, the added value of SnCF Global is to increase the participation of private investors and ensure the monitoring and third-party certification of the results achieved.
16. The TA component of the programme will contribute to making subnational processes more proactive, which is one of the key barriers in the underfunding of these projects.
17. TAP considers that the paradigm-shift potential at the global level could be high in the event of successful implementation of the programme.

## 1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

18. In the event of successful implementation of the programme, the contribution of subnational projects (waste management, street lighting, renewable energy, energy efficiency, etc.) to the sustainable development of regions will be significant, also considering the strengthened monitoring and certification the results.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.27/02/Add.12 Page 136

19. Ensuring and strengthening the sustainable development potential of the programme and SnCF Global is the direct responsibility of the TA component of the programme, which should be implemented through a separate funding proposal led by IUCN as the AE. In particular, the third subcomponent of the TA component (tools and metrics) is responsible for development of the MRV system and for establishing the third-party certification process for the projects and monitoring the results of SnCF Global.
20. In particular, Gold Standard, which is an EE in both funding proposals (TA and SnCF Global), is responsible for the development of sector-related general monitoring tools and indicators for monitoring climate mitigation results and adaptation co-benefits as well as related SDGs. Moreover, the same will be done for each project taking into consideration the project's specificities. These metrics will be also applied for the initial screening of subnational projects as criteria for investment deal identification, selection and, later, for monitoring of results. According to the funding proposal, these metrics will go beyond 'do no harm' and actively ensure that safeguarding, resilience, SDG targets, and nature become integral parts of climate-friendly infrastructure investments in order to create social, ecosystem and resilience gains in addition to GHG mitigation results.
21. In addition to climate change mitigation and adaptation co-benefits (SDG 13), these subnational projects should contribute to several SDGs. In particular, the sample projects provided as examples will contribute to:
4. (a) SDG 3 - good health and well-being. Results from the implementation of waste and wastewater treatment projects should reduce the number of illnesses from hazardous chemicals and those from air, water and soil pollution and contamination;
5. (b) SDG 7 - affordable and clean energy. Planning investments in renewable energy and energy efficiency should facilitate substantial increases in the share of renewable energy in the global energy mix, improve the global rate of energy efficiency, and promote investment in energy infrastructure and clean energy technology; and
6. (c) SDG 11 - make cities inclusive, safe, resilient and sustainable: Subnational projects could enhance inclusive and sustainable urbanization, increase the capacity of local/subnational authorities in sustainable planning and management of human settlement, and reduce the adverse per capita environmental impact of cities, including by paying special attention to air quality and municipal and other waste management.
22. Activity E6.4 and outcome G3 of the equity funding proposal are dedicated to the MRV of results in terms of climate change, SDGs and nature-based solutions.

## 1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

23. Based on its experience and knowledge of barriers to the implementation of subnational projects, TAP confirms that the needs of developing countries to activate underfinanced subnational projects are very high. Despite formally decentralized processes, subnational infrastructure development activities have not always secured the financial and regulatory support of national/central governments, and so have stalled in many developing countries. In this process, intensive consultations with national governments on country/region-specific barriers and potential projects are crucial. However, a consultation process with national governments is not sufficiently evidenced from the funding proposal or the noobjection letters (NOLs) submitted.
24. The alignment of this programme with nationally determined contributions (NDCs) is briefly described for about 30 countries in annex 24 to the funding proposal. TAP fully recognizes the importance of intended nationally determined contributions (NDCs) in climate change decision-making process. However, NDCs are more national or federal strategies, which

<!-- image -->

- are not necessarily relevant and comprehensive enough for investors to analyse countries' needs and barriers to climate change investments at the subnational, sectoral and project level. For such a detailed assessment of the alignment of activities with INDCs, the implementation plans should be used. These are currently being developed but are not yet ready for most countries.
25. The non-bankability of projects and limited access to financial sources due to the small size of subnational activities were identified as key barriers to investing in subnational projects in the first submission of the funding proposal for B.26. TAP expressed concern that, without a preliminary market study (demand and barriers) at the country and sector level, it would not be possible to determine the needs of the recipients. During the assessment process, TAP shared its concern with the AEs regarding barrier analysis. The above-mentioned two barriers are valid, but there are several other barriers that prevent projects from becoming bankable (affordability for the population, enabling environment with relevant regulations that usually are decided at the national level, etc.). Before the B.27 submission, the AE conducted a preliminary barrier analysis of key sectors that SnCF Global would plan to consider for investing in 40 countries (in 4 regions).
26. Some of the key barriers identified at this stage of the preliminary assessment, reported by the AEs, are listed below. TAP recognizes that some of these barriers exist not only at the subnational level but at the national level as well:
- (a) Transaction costs for relatively small subnational projects are critical, and high transaction costs dramatically reduce their bankability. The programme targets this barrier through the TA component;
- (b) In most cases, projects are designed by an equipment provider and not always adapted to the local circumstances. Comprehensive feasibility studies will be conducted by the TA component for the projects in which it is decided to invest;
- (c) Participatory processes are often performed after the project design has been completed. This leaves little flexibility regarding inputs from citizens and local authorities. Intensive consultations with national and local authorities and other stakeholders, as well as capacity-building of subnational capacities in the planning and establishment of an enabling environment for implementation of subnational projects, is the responsibility of the grant component (TA);
- (d) Most public grant programmes and city-to-city cooperation initiatives do not usually fund projects with a cost greater than USD 5 million. SnCF Global will be limited to investing in subnational projects with a total cost of from USD 5 million to USD 75 million;
- (e) Many project developers struggle to find equity partners, and potential debt providers request high equity ratios due to the perceived risks. The programme mainly targets overcoming this barrier;
- (f) Regarding renewable energy, key barriers identified are: lack of appropriate regulation, lack of clarity for independent power producer schemes, and lack of bankable off-takers;
- (g) For the municipal waste management sector, key barriers identified are: lack of financial resources in local/municipal budgets; absence of regulations; existing informal sector; scattered cities and low waste volumes for traditional waste infrastructure in more 'rural' areas; and transportation costs that are too great to be supported by traditional business models; and
- (h) For energy efficiency projects and the energy service company approach, key barriers identified are: energy service company models are not always compliant with public

<!-- image -->

- sector regulations; energy prices are often subsidized; EE projects are often smaller and not attractive for private investors.
27. It is the opinion of TAP that the needs of the recipients (subnational authorities, private sector, citizens, etc.) regarding subnational projects are high, and that the programme is well aware of these needs.

## 1.5 Country ownership Scale: Uncertain

28. TAP was not able to assess the country ownership of this programme due to the following reasons:
2. (a) Actual participant countries have not been identified at this stage, and the country allocations provided in an annex are theoretical. Regional-level preliminary allocations are provided in section C.1, which are also theoretical;
3. (b) The programme applies the 'blind pool' concept, which means that final recipient countries, sectors and particular projects are not identified at the stage of programme submission and assessment by TAP. Thus, the only way to assess country ownership is to obtain more information from NOLs provided by countries. NOLs from 40 countries have been submitted by the AEs to the GCF Secretariat and TAP. The NOLs have provided by countries from four regions: Asia-Pacific, Latin America and the Caribbean, Mediterranean, and sub-Saharan Africa;
4. (c) The observation by TAP is that the format of the NOL is fully standardized at the level of three or four obligatory requirements, and the country's attitude to the project or opinion why the project is important for the country is either not expressed or is expressed only in very exceptional cases. After reviewing all 40 of these NOLs, TAP is not in a position to identify whether countries support the concept of the creation of this equity fund as such or see any significant benefit for their own countries. Moreover, this programme is not reflected in countries' programmes and not registered in the AEs' working programmes; and
5. (d) The maximum number of projects that could be financed by SnCF Global is 35, and, of relevance, not all countries that provide an NOL will ultimately receive an investment from SnCF Global (due to uncertainties and limitations imposed by markets, sponsors, timing, economic developments, etc.), although the programme guarantees that all countries will receive TA financing to build their capacity and improve the market for future investments.
29. At this stage of the assessment, TAP could not assess country ownership. However, among the criteria of SnCF Global for project selection is the request for a supporting letter from subnational authorities. This could strengthen the country ownership process. It is the opinion of TAP that SnCF Global should request letters from both national and subnational authorities for the implementation of each particular project.

## 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Low

30. The efficiency and effectiveness of this programme depend significantly on the implementation arrangements. As described in the section on impact potential, the programme is submitted through two independent funding proposals and by two independent AEs. Such an implementation modality, with unclear legal responsibilities of these AEs to each other and the absence of one AE responsible to GCF, would be the first of its kind for GCF. This has raised concerns on the potential risk of failure of the programme in the event of low liability or not enough experience on the part of one of the partners of the AEs. As this implementation

<!-- image -->

- arrangement would be the first of its kind in GCF practice (at least, as known to TAP), TAP decided to make a review of the available Board policy documents and decisions in order to make an appropriate decision.
31. TAP carefully studied the options offered to the Board by the Secretariat in the document 'Policy on programmatic approaches', which is pending (not yet discussed by the Board). TAP found that, for all options considered within this policy paper, there is always one entity that is responsible and accountable to GCF. Moreover, in the option 'Modality 3: Multiple accredited entities modality ', the document offers the consideration that:
- (a) 'under this modality while there will be separate project-related roles and responsibilities for each AE that is co-implementing parts of the project or programme, there is also a need for a coordinating AE to be responsible for the overall management of the programme, including coordination between programme partners and holistic reporting on the project or programme progress, results and learning.'
32. Taking into consideration the options provided within the afore-mentioned document, including recommendations for strengthening and financial support of the coordination process, TAP concludes that the proposed implementation modality is high risk. This is because the two proposals do not have a clearly identified coordinating AE and are not in line with the options that could be considered by the Board in future.
33. The afore-mentioned document also formulates elements that should be considered for a GCF programmatic approach. TAP has assessed the programme against these elements and concludes that the programme satisfies most of elements. but that two elements are still not fully satisfied by the programme:
- (a) Programmes should be country-driven through observance of NOL procedures and alignment with national priorities. The programme has NOLs from 40 countries. However, TAP is not sure that such standardized NOLs could be enough for supporting multi-country programmes where countries do not see the particular sector and project that will be implemented on the country's territory (see also the section on country ownership). It is the opinion of TAP that, in addition to this general NOL supporting the establishment of SnCF Global, a project support letter for each individual project should be provided by countries at this late stage; and
- (b) Programmatic approach modalities should be developed to foster collaboration among multiple AEs and to promote scale and maximize impact, but at the same time the modalities should address issues that are likely to impede outcomes, performance, efficiency and management. Notably, programmes should incentivize an AE to play a coordinating role for multi-agency initiatives. The programme as submitted satisfies the first part of these requirements but not the requirement on coordination (one AE being responsible).
34. In initial submission to B.26, the concept of SnCF Global was presented as a 'blind pool' without preliminary market and barrier analysis. The GHG reduction potential was calculated on the basis of theoretical projects. At that time, the programme was quite unclear and planned to cover all results areas of GCF programmes without preliminary assessment of market demand, barriers, potential projects and priority countries for starting the programme. TAP has taken into consideration the fact that this concept of 'blind pool' is new and has a high risk (including an implementation modality through two individual funding proposals), and has decided not to recommend this programme to the Board for approval, also having in mind the elements of the programmatic approach from the document 'Policy on programmatic approaches'.
35. In this submission to B.27, the 'pool' is not fully 'blind', the AEs have provided demand analysis of subnational projects' markets and relevantly revised priority results areas. It was

<!-- image -->

- found by the AEs that market demand at the subnational level is not equal for all GCF results areas. Thus, the previous approach of considering 'all results areas' was revised accordingly to the consider those results areas where demand for equity investment is clearer. In its resubmitted version, the programme focuses mainly on sectors related to GHG mitigation, as listed above. Adaptation co-benefits of mitigation projects and contribution to the SDGs will be monitored and certified at the project and programme levels. In addition, the preliminary barrier analysis conducted by the AEs demonstrates to the programme management the barriers that are most critical to subnational activities, and also demonstrates that limitation in required investments because of the medium or small size of subnational projects is not the only barrier that makes this sector unattractive for private investors. These studies have contributed to formulating the project selection criteria, for which further due diligence will be conducted and feasibility studies prepared by the TA component.
36. As mentioned above, along with the 'blind pool' concept, another concern for TAP was, and still is, the implementation arrangement. Practically, this modality does not have an entity that is clearly responsible to GCF, one that will fully coordinate and control the programme. Although it is said in the funding proposals that the equity funding proposal is the leading one, this statement is not supported by a relevant legal design of the implementation process. It is the opinion of TAP that the TA funding proposal is a component of the programme, which should be led by the management of the equity funding proposal. As already mentioned above, the TA proposal may not have any impact and reach any results independently and, therefore, could not be and has not been independently assessed by TAP.
37. In the current arrangement, the liaison parties between these two funding proposals are two entities (R20 and Gold Standard) with very clear EE functions in each funding proposal, and the 'consortium' facilitating cooperation among all AEs and EEs. The 'consortium' is a common platform for both of the funding proposals, but it is not a legal entity that could be responsible to GCF. As reported above, the consortium provides SnCF Global with diverse expertise through these AEs and EEs.
38. In response to the enquiry by TAP, the AEs provided a document, 'Implementation Arrangement', that confirms that these two AEs are fully independent and are responsible to the GCF Secretariat only for their own funding proposal.
39. It is the opinion of TAP that, in order to reduce the implementation complexity and increase the efficiency and effectiveness of the whole programme and SnCF Global, the AE for the TA funding proposal (IUCN) should also be part of the equity funding proposal and accountable to the AE of the equity funding proposal. In particular, IUCN should be included in the equity proposal as an EE that executes, carries out or implements a funded activity with a clear reporting function to the AE of the equity funding proposal (functions other than reporting could also be defined). Reports on the TA component should be submitted to the AE of the equity funding proposal and should be cleared by this entity for disbursements from GCF. An exception could be only the first disbursement. Such an implementation arrangement could reduce the risk that the TA component would produce feasibility studies that would not have investors and reduce the risk of SnCF Global facing a lack of bankable projects.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.27/02/Add.12

Page 141

## II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

40. TAP recognizes that implementation of two components (TA and equity fund) of one programme by two independent AEs through two independent funding proposals without a party that is clearly responsible to GCF entails high risk, and that the proposed structure is not in line with the guidelines that the draft decision 'Policy on programmatic approaches' contemplates as effective for programmatic approaches. TAP also considers that this submission is the first of its kind with a programmatic approach using a 'blind pool', which, practically, could not be assessed against all six investment criteria. However, at the same time, TAP confirms the urgency of the need for a proactive approach for financing subnational activities, and considers the added value of the programme to establish results monitoring and a third-party certification process for the private sector investments. In view of the foregoing, TAP recommends the programme to the Board for consideration.
41. In the event of a positive decision, TAP would make the following recommendations to the Board:
3. (a) Make approval conditional on the revision of the implementation arrangements. In particular, IUCN should be included in the equity funding proposal as an EE with a clear reporting function to the AE of the equity funding proposal (functions other than reporting could also be defined as necessary). Reports on the TA component should be submitted to the AE of the equity funding proposal, and should be cleared by this entity for disbursements from GCF, a sole exception could be the first disbursement; and
4. (b) Before the first disbursement:
5. (i) SnCF Global should be legally established,
6. (ii) All EEs should provide operational manuals including

:

- for Pegasus, updated sections on the structure and operation of SnCF Global, and on participation conditions for the private sector and for the public sector,
- for IUCN and R20, methodologies for a market/demand study, sectoral barrier analysis, establishment of final criteria for selection of subnational projects and for conducting feasibility studies; and
- for Gold Standard, sectoral tools and methodologies for monitoring, reporting and third-party certification by priority sectors of SnCF Global.
42. TAP recognizes that the conditions recommended to the Board in the event of a positive decision might be more particular than usual, due to the uncertainty contained in a 'blind pool' fund structure and approach of the programme. Nevertheless, to enhance the clarity of the programme's operation, TAP agrees it would be critical for the AEs to seek Board consideration of the proposed two AEs' implementation arrangements prior to undertaking further investment.

<!-- image -->

## Reply to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel assessment findings (FP152)

Proposal name:

Global Sub national Climate Fund (SnCF Global) - Equity; Technical Assistance (TA) Facility for the Global Subnational Climate Fund. (submitted separately)

Accredited entity:

Pegasus Capital Advisors LP (Pegasus);

International Union for Conservation of Nature and Natural Resources (IUCN)

## Impact potential

As noted by iTAP, targeted climate finance for subnational level projects is a gap that needs to be addressed for countries to reach their targets under their NDCs.  The AEs have taken on a novel approach with the GCF by bringing together needed abilities from the network of very different entities that are accredited to the GCF-IUCN for the technical assistance work and Pegasus Capital for the equity fund. These AEs will be working with a consortium of agencies as noted in the funding proposals and, in line with lessons learned, experiences and guidance for achieving impact, coordination arrangements between the AEs is formalized through an Implementation Framework Agreement.   This agreement ensures that both Pegasus Capital and IUCN partake in the Steering Committee of the proposed structure as well as engage in the selection of subnational projects that look likely to receive investment financing and that would benefit most from technical assistance. This implementation arrangement is designed to ensure that the anticipated impact is indeed achieved as targeted in the results framework, and will be monitored and reported to the GCF Secretariat in accordance with GCF's monitoring and evaluation framework.

Furthermore, Pegasus, IUCN and the other consortium members have been working collaboratively for almost a year to prepare and refine the two funding proposals, including responding to numerous comments from the Secretariat and iTAP, preparing for the fundraise, obtaining NOLs, hosting webinars, developing the pipeline and otherwise preparing SnCF for success pending Board approval.  This cooperative effort has been outlined in an Implementation Framework Agreement, and the experience over the past year should mitigate concerns about implementation risk.

The pipeline is showing a strong GHG mitigation potential that will be ensured through capacity building to improve project design and pathways to results using technical assistance financing. These results will be certified through a standardized MRV system via Gold Standard.

## Paradigm shift potential

AE concurs with iTAP assessment and remarks.

## Sustainable development potential

AE concurs with iTAP assessment and remarks.

## Needs of the recipient

<!-- image -->

AE concurs with iTAP assessment and remarks.

## Country ownership

Country ownership is not only evidenced from the 40+ countries that have endorsed the proposed structure for addressing climate finance at their subnational levels, but also from regular consultations via the NDAs in each country.  All countries that have endorsed the funding proposals will receive technical assistance support. The aim is to ensure that all countries have the benefit of understanding what is needed to develop a bankable project that can receive investment.   Provision of technical assistance support to all countries increases the 'bankability' of subnational projects that would qualify for investment.   Already, consultations are held on a regular basis to discuss the opportunities in the different sectors of focus where investment will be made available.  Engagement with subnational actors is envisaged throughout the implementation process. Should more projects be ready for investment than those planned from the USD 750 million from the Fund, further engagement will be undertaken with those providing climate and other finance to ensure a sustained momentum for investment in subnational projects delivering mitigation benefits.

We believe iTAP's request for additional NOLs on a subproject-by-subproject basis is not necessary and conflicts with the fund model for SnCF that is at the core of both its paradigm shift potential and efficiency and effectiveness.  The existing NOLs approve investment by SnCF in the host countries. The funding proposals set forth the types of projects SnCF will pursue based on the investment criteria for selection of such projects, which include, among other things, that individual projects be aligned with host country priorities, including the NDCs.  Obtaining additional NOLs for each subproject would be unduly burdensome and delay SnCF's ability to deploy capital.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

iTAP noted that efficiency and effectiveness depends on the implementation arrangement. The approach taken builds on the abilities of two independent AEs, yet coordinating with each other to achieve a higher impact than would be possible if each worked on their own in what they would have traditionally done. The implementation modality is further strengthened following engagement with the GCF to have an Implementation Framework agreement so that on a legal basis, responsibilities for delivery of results are clear.  iTAP has requested that this agreement shows one point of accountability across the agencies, and as a result of this advice and further engagement with the GCF Secretariat, Pegasus Capital is identified as accountable for delivery of results coordinated across the projects with clear roles and responsibilities within each project.  This will also enable holistic reporting to be efficient, as advised by iTAP. This has now been integrated into a strengthened Implementation Framework agreement.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

The AEs would like to thank iTAP for recognizing the high potential of the projects in creating a paradigm shift and for sustainable development, while being rated high for responsiveness to needs of the recipients. This response is provided to further explain areas where questions were raised and have now been clarified.

## 1. Overview

The Subnational Climate Fund (SnCF) Gender Assessment serves as the foundation to strengthen the programme's (Fund and Technical Assistance) responsiveness to the multiple, culturally-derived principles of gender equality and women's empowerment , and to address the links between gender equality, climate mitigation and adaptation, and environmental sustainability in the context of a global climate equity fund and related Technical Assistance. The Assessment informs the accompanying Gender Action Plan , both at fund and project level, with indicators and targets to support SnCF commitments to and engagement with women, men, girls and boys, including those from marginalized communities i , in compliance with GCF and SnCF gender policies. The Assessment and Action Plan support the SnCF Environmental Social Management System (ESMS).

The Assessment builds upon quantitative data and qualitative evidence derived from field experience (including 1:1 household interviews), industry surveys, and desk review of existing literature and secondary databases, as noted in the Sources section. Like the ESMS, the SnCF Gender Assessment and Action Plan are living documents and will be regularly updated to reflect key developments, including:

1. Portfolio : The SnCF is a Fund of projects that may operate in up to 40 countries (to date SnCF has received 37 non-objection letters -NOLs). The specific portfolio of funded projects, and indeed the countries in which those projects may occur, are yet to be confirmed. Relevant country-specific, sex-disaggregated gender data must be assessed once the prospective project pipeline and host countries become known.
2. COVID-19: The full impact in the long term of the global COVID virus is unknown. In the short term, findings have already suggested that it has had differentiated impacts in the lives of men and women. With respect to reproductive work, women and girls, already disproportionately responsible for household duties, including water, food and fuel collection; food preparation; and taking care of children as well as elderly and ill relatives, have experienced heightened levels of responsibility in the midst of the pandemic and subsequent stay-at-home orders. In addition, home quarantines, market impacts and food shortages have also been found to increase household tensions and, as a result, women have been exposed to higher risks of domestic violence. The Assessment and Action Plan will be updated as virus-related development, people differentiated impacts and strategies for remediation become more clear (see Section 2.2).

## SnCF Gender Assessment

## 1.1 Country Ownership

The Fund investment focus is sub-national climate projects. However, the SnCF fully adheres to the principle of country ownership. The Gender-responsive Assessment and Action Plans for every funded project will align with prevailing international ratified legal frameworks, regional and national gender policies, or other public policy for gender equality and women's empowerment . The assessment and action plan will seek also to align the project with other national development strategies that promote equal opportunities, poverty reduction and sustainable use of natural resources whether in the intervention region or the sector. This will include analysis of the gender equality landscape and data relevant to the host country and in the targeted sector(s), including Nationally Determined Commitments (NDCs). ii

## 1.2 COVID Assessment

The SnCF will begin implementation before the impacts of the global COVID-19 virus crisis are fully known. However, it is clear that the impacts will be felt deeply in the host countries in which the SnCF plans to invest (see Graphic 1, below). Of particular relevance is the SnCF subnational focus, as over 90% of COVID cases occur within urban settings. With respect to gender-related impacts, the Committee for the Coordination of Statistical Activities (UN-CCSA) concludes that COVID-19 will disproportionately impact the vulnerable and compound existing inequalities. Impacts may include food shortages, mobility restrictions, decreased access to health care and education, higher unemployment, and increases in domestic violence. ' iii

Graphic 1 : Confirmed cases of COVID-19, May 2020

<!-- image -->

The COVID19 pandemic is threatening fragile gains on gender equality and women's rights. As countries respond to the crisis, sex-disaggregated data is critical to examine the differential impact on women and men towards evidence-based decision-making. Globally, women on average do three times as much unpaid care and domestic work as men -a workload that intensifies with school closures and when health systems are overloaded. Additionally, research has begun to surface indicating that women are more vulnerable to losing their jobs than men in response to the crisis. Widespread job losses will have long-term impacts for women's economic independence and security. With 740 million women globally in informal employment and a majority employed in services, women are being, and will continue to be, particularly hard hit by the impacts of the COVID crisis. iv Indigenous communities and specifically women and children are also more vulnerable due to overcrowded housing, unsafe water, and poor access to healthcare. v

The SnCF Gender-responsive Assessment and Action Plan will be updated on a regular basis to reflect the impacts of COVID in host countries and related project sectors.

## 2. Project Category Assessment

As previously noted, the SnCF will be a fund of projects that may operate in up to 40 countries. The specific portfolio of funded projects, and indeed the countries in which those projects will occur, have yet to be confirmed. At such time as host countries and prospective pipeline projects are identified, SnCF will seek country-specific, sex-disaggregated data by project type to inform gender-responsive project design and implementation. This activity will be supported by framework guidelines on gender assessment, to ensure that project proposal and implementation mainstream gender.

In the current absence of country-specific data, the SnCF has conducted peer-project Assessments to help identify the drivers of change and the gender dynamics needed to achieve the fund's project adaptation and mitigation goals. The SnCF will invest in the following project categories (all Category B -lower risk):

1. Energy Efficiency
2. Renewable energy (small scale solar and wind, no hydro)
3. Waste optimization -biochar, compost, recycling
4. Water and Sanitation
5. Regenerative Agriculture / Aquaculture (Nature based solutions)
6. Urban design solutions

SnCF has assessed each project categories beginning with the Gender-Related Vulnerabilities and Capacities for Change in GCF Projects. vi In addition, the Assessment is framed using the Six Domains of Gender Analysis Framework Approach vii including:

1. Access - to resources, income, services, employment, information, and benefits, poverty rate, formal / informal employment, unemployment, division of labour;
2. Knowledge, Beliefs, and Perception - commonly held beliefs, perceptions, and stereotypes, mobility, vulnerable groups;
3. Practices and participation - adult literacy rate, skills training and gaps, attendance at meetings and training courses, and accepting or seeking out services;
4. Time and Space - how time is spent and how men and women each contribute to the welfare of the family, community, and their society;
5. Legal rights and status -legal status of women, access to healthcare, access to and management of resources (economic, financial, physical, natural, other assets), access to ownership and recourse;
6. Power and Decision Making -existing gender inequalities that may be exacerbated by project impacts, anticipated differences in vulnerability and adaptive capacity viii , use of household and individual economic resources, choice of employment.

## 2.1 Energy Efficiency and Renewable Energy

Table 1: - GCF Result Area Energy Access and Power Generation ix

| GCF - Result Area                  | GCF - Vulnerabilities                                                                                                         | GCF - Capacities for Change                                                                                                                                                                                        |
|------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Energy access and Power generation | Share of women and women- headed households without energy access Impacts women are experiencing due to lack of energy access | Roles of women in household energy supply/use Share of women and men in the energy labour market and type of occupation Policies and institutional framework to promote gender equality in energy/power generation |

Energy access is fundamental for women's economic empowerment.  For example solar water pumps, electrical grill, cooling, solar electricity, solar water heating all open the possibility for women micro and small enterprises (MSEs) to diversify livelihoods and reduce work load. [Source: 2017, UNDP, Gender and Sustainable Energy x ] Energy efficiency and renewable energy projects have distinct gender characteristics that have the potential to disproportionately affect women and girls. For example, women and girls are often primarily responsible for collecting fuel and water at the community level. Also, poor women tend to participate in the informal economic sector (for example, the food sector), which relies strongly on biomass as its main energy source. This link and impact, however, is not recognized on a policy and programming level, leading to the gender impact not featuring heavily in national energy policies and priorities.

The project assessment below focuses on a clean cookstove example.

Indoor pollution from the burning of solid fuels is also a serious health problem for women and girls. Every year, 4.3 million people -mainly women and children, those most likely to be near the burning or because indoor air pollution is linked to cooking, a role predominantly held by women -die because of indoor air pollution. In 2012 alone, 7 million people died -representing one in eight of total global deaths -because of exposure to indoor air pollution, which confirms that air pollution is now the world's largest single environmental health risk. Women are also exposed to other health risks linked to the work of fuelwood / energy collection. Much of the work requires high levels of physical energy, however their access to nutrition within the household is often less than other family members. Women's poor nutrition vis -à-vis their workloads increases their susceptibility to anaemia and perinatal mortality, while the heavy lift of energy collection could result in postnatal complications and takes a toll on women's well -being.  Moreover, genderdifferentiated roles related to land and resources can also make women more vulnerable to gender-based violence (GBV) while carrying out daily responsibilities, as seen in firewood and water collection activities. xi

Energy poverty also affects women and girls ' time, resulting in 'time poverty' (a lack of time for rest and leisure after taking into account the time spent working, whether in the labour market or at home). Women spend considerable time gathering biomass for energy. Because women are often responsible for these activities, climate-induced scarcity of natural resources can exacerbate their time poverty. As women are forced to travel and spend more time collecting these resources, they lose out on other, self-fulfilling activities (such as education, when otherwise available). A study conducted in India, Bangladesh and Nepal found that women in South Asia spend up to 20 or more hours per week in energy collection. Better access to modern energy can help alleviate some of these challenges. Research in Brazil also shows that girls in rural areas with access to electricity are 59 percent more likely to complete primary education by the time they are 18 years old than those without.

Improved cookstoves can reduce cooking time by 50 percent and increase fuel efficiency by 30 percent -high efficiency cookstoves lead to even larger benefits in time and energy savings, while also contributing to emissions reductions. A preliminary analysis of mitigation pledges under the Paris Agreement shows that some 32 countries have included efficient cooking initiatives in their INDCs.

In the formal sector, women generally have less access to finances and energy-related services than men. Studies from the Africa region, for example, show that women-headed businesses generally face more impediments than men in accessing grid electricity. Experiences in Ethiopia, Ghana, Kenya, Tanzania and Zambia suggest that women entrepreneurs also face greater discrimination than men in the form of delays in obtaining electrical connections and the expectation that they will pay bribes for these services. More broadly, women are often excluded from discussions about energy plans and policies across scales, despite being primary household-energy managers, limiting their entry into the energy industry and resulting in gender-blind energy project planning, financing, execution and implementation.

## Specific Project Assessment: Cookstoves in Brazil -Gender Responsive certification, challenges and opportunities xii

The introduction of the SDG framework and application of Gold Standard for the Global Goals offers new opportunities and tools to promote and measure outcomes in terms of gender equality. At the same time, achieving Gender Responsive certification requires a more comprehensive and precise approach to project monitoring.
